<https://www.kaggle.com/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection?utm_medium=email&utm_source=gamma&utm_campaign=comp+rsna+aug+2026>
and we have to submit a proper model check and verify the rules and analyze each and everything the scores and etc details what can we do better i have collab and i have a team of 5 i want the best possible and take advatnage of each and everything to the very best of your abbility detail analysis strictly and i want the best and top score which can win and etc
and what can i do to improve and be better and etc

Read 2 pages, searched the web
I couldn’t read the official Kaggle rules page directly, because it loads with JavaScript and only returned metadata. This analysis is built from the competition’s overview text as quoted in search results and from several public team repos and write-ups. Read the Rules tab yourselves before relying on any rule below.

Timing
Today is Oct 12. Per one public repo, the entry/merger deadline is 15 Oct and the final submission deadline is 22 Oct, with 5 submissions per day. You have 3 days to finalize the team and about 10 days to finish. Plan around that.
github

Rules and constraints
Format: it’s a code competition. Notebooks run for at most 9 hours with internet off. Inference runs on Kaggle’s dual T4 GPUs, and the reports aren’t available at test time. So the model must work from MRI alone.
GitHub - homeshwarnelakurthi/RSNA-Knee-Abnormality-Detection: RSNA Knee Abnormality Detection Create a model that can detect knee abnormalities based on multimodal imaging data · GitHub +2
Prizes: $77,000 across a leaderboard track and an efficiency track.
github
Data security: one team’s notes say Rule 4.b may forbid sending report text to hosted LLM APIs, and the host hasn’t ruled. Don’t send reports to any hosted API, including me. Use open-weights models run inside Kaggle or locally, or ask the host in the discussion forum.
github
P100 GPU: one repo warns that the P100 fails at the first convolution, so pick the T4.
github
Code license: one repo notes CC-BY-NC 4.0 for winning code. Check the rules for this and for external pretrained models and data.
github
Team size: I couldn’t confirm the maximum. Check it on the Rules page, since you’re a team of 5.
Metric
The score is the macro-averaged ROC AUC across 12 targets, so every finding counts equally. These are ligament and meniscus tears, osteoarthritis in three compartments, effusion, synovitis, Baker’s cyst, contusion and fracture. Weak findings drag the average down more than strong ones lift it.
kaggle

AUC only depends on ranking, so calibration doesn’t matter. Rank-averaging ensemble members is therefore the right way to blend.

The data problem
There are 4,407 training studies, about 24,371 series, about 820,000 slices, from 19 sites.
github
Only 58 studies have gold labels. The rest only have multilingual radiology reports.
github
Gold labels are image-derived by two radiologists plus an adjudicator, with severity thresholds. Report-derived labels agree only about 82%, and the gap is systematic.
github
The best public report-label sets reach about 0.89 macro AUC against the gold labels. Synovitis is the worst, at 0.79 or lower.
github
So the competition is two problems: turning reports into good labels, then training image models on them. One repo’s summary puts it bluntly: label quality is the competition, and the vision model is secondary. The 58 gold studies should be a validation set only. They are balanced on purpose, so don’t train on them.
github

Where the leaderboard stands
As of Oct 6, with 5,314 teams:

Gold (top 20) needed about 0.958 or higher on the public board, silver about 0.945, and bronze about 0.944.
github
About 1,200 teams sit at 0.943–0.944, forks of the same public stack. Forking it earns nothing.
github
Public CoAtNet-based notebooks report about 0.949 single-model AUC.
github
The official pages warn that finding prevalence may differ across train, public and private sets. Expect a shake-up, and don’t overfit the public score.
huggingface
What I’d do to beat the pack
Better labels. Fuse several independent report-label sources, such as multiple open-weights LLM extractors plus public CC0 label sets. Check each one per finding against the 58 gold studies. One public pipeline does exactly this, fusing a primary extractor, a second-family cross-check model and a third public label set. Use soft probabilities rather than hard labels.
huggingface
Diverse strong image models. A blend member has to be close in strength to the stack to help. One team’s 0.86 model lowered the stack from 0.943 to 0.937, and they concluded their model needed about 0.92 or better before blending. So aim for diverse models around 0.93 or higher: ConvNeXt, CoAtNet and EfficientNetV2 backbones, at higher resolution (384px).
github
Study-level architecture. Use a 2.5D backbone with 3-slice windows across the sagittal, coronal and axial series. Add a transformer to mix windows across series, and use attention pooling per finding. Fold-wise training with the 58 gold studies as validation worked for others.
Per-finding blend weights. The macro metric lets you weight models per finding. For example, image models beat report labels on effusion, Baker’s cyst, contusion and fracture, but are weak on small structures like MCL. Use higher resolution there.
Training stability. One team saw 3 of 4 folds diverge from too high a learning rate, then fixed it with lr 1.5e-4 and a warm-up. Use warm-up and watch the gold-set AUC every epoch.
Inference safety. Add a time-budget guard, because a notebook that times out scores nothing. Keep a spare notebook that runs end to end.
Efficiency prize (optional). It’s scored on AUC against a benchmark and on runtime. Decide early whether to chase it with a separate small model, or ignore it.
Splitting five people
Person Job
1 Label pipeline: open-weights LLM extraction, multilingual handling, fusion, per-finding checks against gold
2 Preprocessing and caching: orientation, resampling, 256px and 384px caches
3 Model A: ConvNeXt or CoAtNet, study-level
4 Model B: EfficientNetV2 or another diverse backbone
5 Inference notebook, time budget, rank-blend, submission hygiene, final-pick strategy
Timeline:

Oct 12–15: confirm team and rules, lock labels v1, start caching.
Oct 16–19: train models, validate on gold, test blends.
Oct 20–21: freeze training, build and test the full inference notebook.
Oct 22: pick two final submissions, one safe and one bolder. Don’t change anything last-minute.
Colab and compute
The imaging data is about 570 GB and stays on Kaggle, so don’t try to download it to Colab. Train and cache on Kaggle, and use Colab only for label experiments and small tests. Kaggle GPU quotas are limited, so split the work across team accounts.
github

Honest expectations
Gold needs about 0.958 or better, versus 0.944 for the common stack. That is a big gap, and the ranking can shuffle at the private reveal. I can’t promise a top finish. A realistic target is a medal-level score through better labels, diverse strong models and a clean blend.

What I need from you
What is your current best public score, and what does your pipeline look like?
Which parts are done: labels, preprocessing, models?
Is the team already merged, and what’s the max team size on the Rules page?
With those answers I can give you specific code and a concrete plan for the next 10 days.

I analyzed your leaderboard CSV (5,814 teams) along with the rules and pages you pasted.

What the leaderboard shows
Rank Public score
1 0.966
10 0.963
20 0.962
50 0.959
100 0.956
265 0.954
531 0.954
1000 0.950
651 teams are at exactly 0.954, and 349 at 0.950. These are forks of the public notebooks. The notebook list is full of “Apex Grandmaster”, “Heliosli blend” and “LB 0.954” forks. Forking one gets you a bronze-range score and no edge over those teams.
Gold-range scores are 0.962 or higher, and #1 is 0.966. The gap from the 0.954 plateau to gold is about 0.008 AUC, which is large at this level.
The public board uses only about 30% of the test data. That is roughly 400 studies, so ranks 10–100 are partly noise (my estimate, not a stated figure). The final standings use the other 70%. Don’t chase the public score by a thousandth, and build for robustness.
Top teams made 180–340 submissions over the competition. Mergers are capped at 5 submissions per day times the days elapsed (about 74 days, so around 370), which is an easy limit to hit if you merge heavy-submission teams.
What I can do
Write and debug the full pipeline code: DICOM preprocessing and caching, 2.5D study-level models, training loops with warm-up and fold CV, and evaluation against the 58 gold studies.
Build the rank-average and per-finding blend scripts, including the optimization of blend weights per finding.
Write the inference notebook with a time-budget guard that always produces a valid submission.csv.
Review any notebook, log or error you paste. I can find bugs, leakage and wasted compute.
Design the label pipeline: prompts and extraction schema for open-weights LLMs, fusion of multiple label sources, and per-finding checks against gold.
Plan the 10 days across your five people, and decide which two submissions to pick as finals.
Prepare the winner deliverables (reproducible code, public weights dataset, video script), which are required if you place.
What I can’t do
I can’t train or run anything on the competition data. My sandbox has no GPU and no Kaggle access, and the data is 570 GB. All training has to run on Kaggle, or on your own hardware or Colab for small tests.
I can’t see your account, notebooks or submissions. You have to paste code, logs and scores.
I can’t see the private leaderboard. Nobody can guarantee a win, and I won’t promise one. A realistic target is the gold-range band, and the rank-1 spot depends on luck at the final reveal.
I can’t use hosted LLMs on the reports. Data Security rule 4.b forbids passing competition data to anyone not in the competition. There is also a pinned forum thread, “Use of Commercially Hosted LLMs”, which I haven’t read. Read it, and use only open-weights models (Qwen3-8B, Gemma 3 and others are on the Models tab) inside Kaggle.
I won’t help with anything that breaks the rules. That means private code sharing between teams, multiple accounts, or hand-labeling test data.
Rule points that affect your plan
15 Oct is both the entry and merger deadline. All five members must have accepted the rules by then. The Kaggle rules also say merging teams must stay within the submission cap.
External data is allowed if it’s publicly and freely available. The OAI knee dataset is in dispute: there are open forum threads on its eligibility and on datasets that need an access application. Don’t build your core on it until the host answers. A pretrained model that is clearly public and free (DINOv2, EfficientNet and others on the Models tab) is lower risk.
Winners must open-source under CC-BY-NC 4.0. They must also publish weights as a public Kaggle dataset and provide a video and reproducible training code. Check licenses on pretrained models you use.
The Efficiency prize is separate and rewards runtime. Skip it unless you have a spare person.
What I need from you
Your best public score. Is it a fork or your own model? Paste the notebook or its structure.
Your team setup. Who has accepted the rules and merged, and how many submissions does each account have?
Your compute. Kaggle GPU hours left per account, plus anything extra you have (Colab Pro, local GPUs).
Your label source. Which report-label dataset are you using, if any, and what is your per-finding AUC against the 58 gold studies?
Your per-finding scores. Which of the 12 findings are weakest?
Your public notebooks. Which ones are you blending, and with what weights?
About Antigravity
Antigravity can help with the workflow, and the Kaggle CLI works well inside it: pushing and pulling notebooks, checking submission status, and managing datasets. It can also run local EDA and small tests on the sample DICOMs. It won’t change the core limit, which is that full training must run on Kaggle’s mounted data. I’d use it for orchestration and code editing, with me for design and review.

Honest bottom line
At 0.954 you’d get a bronze-range result. To reach the 0.962+ range you need something the 651 forks don’t have. The strongest levers are better fused labels, 2–3 diverse models each at 0.93+ on their own, higher resolution for small structures, and a careful per-finding blend. Send me the items above and I’ll turn this into a concrete day-by-day plan with code.

My first read of this notebook was wrong. It does write submission.csv, much further down, so I’ll read the rest before commenting.

Ran a command
I read the full notebook. One correction first, then what it is and what to do.

Correction
Earlier I suspected this notebook never writes submission.csv. That was wrong. The Raptor stage writes raptor_sub.csv and raptor_raw.csv, but a final cell near the bottom blends everything and writes /kaggle/working/submission.csv. So the file is submittable as it stands.

What this notebook is
It is the public ~0.954-class stack, run in three stages:

Raptor stage: the 0.949 CoAtNet from the write-up I summarized earlier. The weights file is named …oai…swa.pt, so it was trained with OAI external knee data. Mirror averaging is included.
“Ours” stage: a fine-tuned OrthoFoundation-L compact model, run through probe_dual.py and compact_infer.py.
Blend stage: a fixed rank blend of 0.7 Raptor and 0.3 ours per finding. The notebook says the weights were not fitted on any labels.
It carries a 2-hour timeout on stage 1 and an 8.3-hour deadline overall, which leaves under an hour of margin against the 9-hour limit. Studies not reached before the deadline fall back to the other model’s ranks, so a slow run degrades instead of failing. A kernel killed at 9 hours would still score nothing.

What your uploaded files do and don’t show
raptor_raw.csv and raptor_sub.csv each contain 3 rows, which is the example test set. The Raptor stage ran and produced plausible probabilities, so the first stage works on your side.
The rank values in raptor_sub.csv are 0, 0.5 and 1 because there are only 3 studies. That is expected and means nothing about accuracy.
No ours_sub.csv or submission.csv was uploaded, so I don’t know whether stages 2 and 3 completed. Their absence is a warning sign but not proof, since you may simply not have uploaded them.
You still have no leaderboard score and no real-set runtime.
Do this now
Confirm that you’ve joined, accepted the rules and verified your identity. Your last pasted page still showed the accept button, and Submit stays disabled until verification is done.
Open your copy of the notebook, make sure internet is off and the GPU is T4 x2, then run Save Version → Save & Run All (Commit).
When it finishes, check the log for three lines in order: RAPTOR_STAGE_DONE, OURS_STAGE_COMPLETE and NOTEBOOK_COMPLETE. The output folder should contain submission.csv.
If you see Cannot resolve <name>, attach the dataset named in that error message. The notebook’s own checks list what it needs: the competition plus the weights and bundle datasets (the …swa-weights…, rsna-knee-compact-v1 and rsna-orthofoundation-fold0-epoch7… ones). Don’t retype or edit the hash values, because the notebook asserts them.
Click Submit from the committed version. When it scores, record the public score and the runtime shown on the Submissions tab.
Risks you should know
OAI. The Raptor weights were trained with OAI data, and the host has not ruled on OAI or OAI-trained weights. For a medal-level result the risk is probably low, but it is real, and a prize-level result would be audited. You have no competition-only fallback. Decide with your team whether you accept that risk, and check the forum thread for a ruling before Oct 22.
You’ll tie a crowd. Hundreds of teams sit at 0.954. This notebook is a baseline, not an advantage.
Don’t edit the model code. The hash checks will fail.
What else could help (low expected gain)
Honestly, I don’t see a reliable improvement I can promise in the time left. Any change to the 0.7/0.3 weights can’t be validated cleanly (Raptor saw all the training data), and the public score is noisy. The safest uses of your remaining daily submissions are small, defensible tests, such as per-finding weight changes, each justified by something you can explain. Expect a gain of about ±0.001–0.003 at best, which is inside the public score’s noise.

Radiological Society of North America · Research Code Competition · 11 days to go

Join Competition
RSNA Knee Abnormality Detection
Create a model that can detect knee abnormalities based on multimodal imaging data

Overview
A single knee scan can reveal a dozen different problems. In this competition, you are tasked to build machine learning models that detect a defined set of clinically important abnormalities on knee MRI examinations.

Start

2 months ago
Close

11 days to go
Merger & Entry
Description
The knee is the most commonly injured and imaged joint in the body. Osteoarthritis alone affects an estimated 654 million people worldwide, while acute knee injuries account for 15 to 40 percent of all sports-related trauma. MRIs show clinicians ligaments, cartilage, menisci, and bone in detail, without exposing patients to radiation.

Reading those scans isn’t always straightforward. ACL and MCL tears, meniscal damage, cartilage loss, fractures, and other abnormalities can be subtle, and radiologists don’t always interpret them the same way. Access to musculoskeletal radiologists is also limited, especially outside major medical centers, leading to delays and inconsistent diagnoses.

In this competition, you will develop multimodal machine learning models to detect twelve clinically important knee abnormalities. You'll work with the first RSNA AI Challenge dataset that pairs every imaging study with its original radiology report, enabling your models to learn from both visual scans and written diagnostic text.

High-performing models can act as robust decision support tools, delivering the accuracy, consistency, and speed needed to elevate expert-level knee MRI interpretation and improve care across disparate clinic settings.

Evaluation
Submissions are evaluated by the average area under the ROC curve between the predicted confidence scores and the observed targets across the twelve targets:

The final score is, in other words, the macro-averaged AUC ROC.

Submission File
For each row in the test set, you must predict a confidence score for each of the twelve target labels. The file should contain a header and have the following format:

StudyInstanceUID,ACL,MCL,Medial Meniscus,Lateral Meniscus,Medial OA,Lateral OA,PF OA,Effusion,Synovitis,Baker's,Contusion,Fracture
<uid_1>,0.5,0.5,0.5,0.5,0.5,0.5,0.5,0.5,0.5,0.5,0.5,0.5
<uid_2>,0.5,0.5,0.5,0.5,0.5,0.5,0.5,0.5,0.5,0.5,0.5,0.5
...

All deadlines are at 11:59 PM UTC on the corresponding day unless otherwise noted. The competition organizers reserve the right to update the contest timeline if they deem it necessary.

Code Requirements

Submissions to this competition must be made through Notebooks. In order for the "Submit" button to be active after a commit, the following conditions must be met:

CPU Notebook <= 9 hours run-time
GPU Notebook <= 9 hours run-time
Internet access disabled
Freely & publicly available external data is allowed, including pre-trained models
Submission file must be named submission.csv
Please see the Code Competition FAQ for more information on how to submit. And review the code debugging doc if you are encountering submission errors.

Efficiency Prize Evaluation
Efficiency Prize
We are hosting a second track that focuses on model efficiency, because highly accurate models are often computationally heavy.

For the Efficiency Prize, we will evaluate submissions on both runtime and predictive performance.

To be eligible for an Efficiency Prize, a submission:

Must be among the submissions selected by a team for the Leaderboard Prize, or else among those submissions automatically selected under the conditions described in the My Submissions tab.
Must be ranked on the Private Leaderboard higher than the sample_submission.csv benchmark.
All submissions meeting these conditions will be considered for the Efficiency Prize. A submission may be eligible for both the Leaderboard Prize and the Efficiency Prize.

An Efficiency Prize will be awarded to eligible submissions according to how they are ranked by the following evaluation metric on the private test data. See the Prizes tab for the prize awarded to each rank. More details may be posted via discussion forum updates.

During the training period of the competition, you may see a leaderboard for the public test data in the following notebook, updated daily: Efficiency Leaderboard. After the competition ends, we will update this leaderboard with efficiency scores on the private data. During the training period, this leaderboard will show only the rank of each team, but not the complete score.

Radiological Society of North America · Research Code Competition · 11 days to go

RSNA Knee Abnormality Detection
Create a model that can detect knee abnormalities based on multimodal imaging data

Dataset Description
Dataset Description
This dataset contains knee MRI studies annotated for twelve common findings: ligament and meniscus injuries, three compartments of osteoarthritis, joint effusion, synovitis, Baker's cyst, bone contusion, and fracture. Each study comprises a collection of individual MRI sequences from a single scanning session formatted as DICOM series. Your task is to predict the per-study probability of each of the twelve findings.

Studies come from a diverse international mix of imaging sites and span a wide range of scanners, protocols, and populations. Only a small subset of training studies carry per-condition labels. We also provide the original text of the radiology report from which you may wish to derive the labels for the remaining studies.

Files
train.csv One row per training study.

StudyInstanceUID - unique identifier for the study; matches the folder name under train_series/.
Report - the free-text radiology report. May be in any of several languages, depending on the reporting institution.
Twelve binary labels:

ACL - anterior cruciate ligament injury (0/1).
MCL - medial collateral ligament injury (0/1).
Medial Meniscus - medial meniscus tear (0/1).
Lateral Meniscus - lateral meniscus tear (0/1).
Medial OA - osteoarthritis of the medial tibiofemoral compartment (0/1).
Lateral OA - osteoarthritis of the lateral tibiofemoral compartment (0/1).
PF OA - patellofemoral osteoarthritis (0/1).
Effusion - joint effusion / excess fluid (0/1).
Synovitis - inflammation of the joint lining (0/1).
Baker's - Baker's cyst (0/1).
Contusion - bone contusion / bone bruise (0/1).
Fracture - fracture (0/1).
train_series.csv One row per training series. Each series is a single MRI acquisition and each study comprises several series.

StudyInstanceUID - study this series belongs to.
SeriesInstanceUID - unique identifier for the series; matches the folder name under train_series/<StudyInstanceUID>/.
Fluid_Sensitive - 1 if the sequence emphasizes fluid signal (T2, PD, STIR, and similar), 0 otherwise.
Fat_Suppression - 1 if the sequence applies fat suppression, 0 otherwise. Note that although Fluid_Sensitive and Fat_Suppression are often correlated, as observed in the training set, they are not necessarily equivalent for every case.
Anatomical_Plane - imaging plane: Sagittal, Coronal, or Axial.
train_series/ Training DICOMs, organized as train_series/<StudyInstanceUID>/<SeriesInstanceUID>/<SOPInstanceUID>.dcm. Each .dcm is a single image slice. Series typically contain 20–45 slices (median 30), with a long tail out to a few hundred.

test.csv Example test file with three study IDs from the public test set. During scoring, this example data will be replaced with the actual test data. There are about 1300 studies in the test set. The Report field will not be provided at the testing stage.

StudyInstanceUID - unique identifier for a test study.
test_series.csv Same schema as train_series.csv, for the example test studies. Replaced with the real test-series descriptors during scoring.

test_series/ Example test DICOMs, same layout as train_series/. Replaced with the real test DICOMs during scoring.

sample_submission.csv A valid submission with all label columns set to 0.5.

Dataset Distribution Notice
Although efforts have been made to ensure each abnormality is represented in each dataset, the prevalence of abnormalities is not guaranteed to be the same across the training, public leaderboard, and final evaluation datasets.

DICOM Notes
Intensities, orientations, and resolutions vary across series and studies. Series come in a mix of transfer syntaxes (uncompressed Explicit VR Little Endian, JPEG Lossless, JPEG 2000, Implicit VR Little Endian). Every DICOM has been stripped to an allowlisted set of 86 metadata tags.

Competition Rules
ENTRY IN THIS COMPETITION CONSTITUTES YOUR ACCEPTANCE OF THESE OFFICIAL COMPETITION RULES.
See Section 3.18 for defined terms

The Competition named below is a skills-based competition to promote and further the field of data science. You must register via the Competition Website to enter. To enter the Competition, you must agree to these Official Competition Rules, which incorporate by reference the provisions and content of the Competition Website and any Specific Competition Rules herein (collectively, the "Rules"). Please read these Rules carefully before entry to ensure you understand and agree. You further agree that Submission in the Competition constitutes agreement to these Rules. You may not submit to the Competition and are not eligible to receive the prizes associated with this Competition unless you agree to these Rules. These Rules form a binding legal agreement between you and the Competition Sponsor with respect to the Competition. Your competition Submissions must conform to the requirements stated on the Competition Website. Your Submissions will be scored based on the evaluation metric described on the Competition Website. Subject to compliance with the Competition Rules, Prizes, if any, will be awarded to Participants with the best scores, based on the merits of the data science models submitted. See below for the complete Competition Rules. For Hackathon Competitions, your Submissions will be judged by the Competition Sponsor based on the evaluation rubric set forth on the Competition Website (“Evaluation Rubric”). The Prizes, if any, will be awarded to Participants with the highest ranking(s) as determined by the Competition Sponsor based on the Evaluation Rubric.

You cannot sign up to Kaggle from multiple accounts and therefore you cannot enter or submit from multiple accounts.

3. COMPETITION-SPECIFIC RULES
In addition to the provisions of the General Competition Rules below, you understand and agree to these Competition-Specific Rules required by the Competition Sponsor:

4. TEAM LIMITS
a. The maximum Team size is five (5). b. Team mergers are allowed and can be performed by the Team leader. In order to merge, the combined Team must have a total Submission count less than or equal to the maximum allowed as of the Team Merger Deadline. The maximum allowed is the number of Submissions per day multiplied by the number of days the competition has been running. For Hackathons, each team is allowed one (1) Submission; any Submissions submitted by Participants before merging into a Team will be unsubmitted.

5. SUBMISSION LIMITS
a. You may submit a maximum of five (5) Submissions per day. b. You may select up to two (2) Final Submissions for judging. c. For Hackathons, each Team may submit one (1) Submission only.

6. COMPETITION TIMELINE
a. Competition Timeline dates (including Entry Deadline, Final Submission Deadline, Start Date, and Team Merger Deadline, as applicable) are reflected on the competition’s Overview > Timeline page.

7. COMPETITION DATA
a. Data Access and Use.

Competition Use and Commercial are checked: You may access and use the Competition Data for any purpose, whether commercial or non-commercial, including for participating in the Competition and on Kaggle.com forums, and for academic research and education subject to the terms and conditions referenced in 4.a.2. below. The Competition Sponsor reserves the right to disqualify any Participant who uses the Competition Data other than as permitted by the Competition Website and these Rules.

The Competition Data is also subject to the following terms and conditions: <http://rsna.org/mira-license>

b. Data Security.

You agree to use reasonable and suitable measures to prevent persons who have not formally agreed to these Rules from gaining access to the Competition Data. You agree not to transmit, duplicate, publish, redistribute or otherwise provide or make available the Competition Data to any party not participating in the Competition. You agree to notify Kaggle immediately upon learning of any possible unauthorized transmission of or unauthorized access to the Competition Data and agree to work with Kaggle to rectify any unauthorized transmission or access.
5. WINNER LICENSE
a. Under Section 2.8 (Winners Obligations) of the General Rules below, you hereby grant and will grant the Competition Sponsor the following license(s) with respect to your Submission if you are a Competition winner:

*Open Source: You hereby license and will license your winning Submission and the source code used to generate the Submission under CC-BY-NC 4.0, an Open Source Initiative-approved license (see <www.opensource.org>) that in no event limits commercial use of such code or model containing or depending on such code.

For generally commercially available software that you used to generate your Submission that is not owned by you, but that can be procured by the Competition Sponsor without undue expense, you do not need to grant the license in the preceding Section for that software.

In the event that input data or pretrained models with an incompatible license are used to generate your winning solution, you do not need to grant an open source license in the preceding Section for that data and/or model(s).

b. You may be required by the Sponsor to provide a detailed description of how the winning Submission was generated, to the Competition Sponsor’s specifications, as outlined in Section 2.8, Winner’s Obligations. This may include a detailed description of methodology, where one must be able to reproduce the approach by reading the description, and includes a detailed explanation of the architecture, preprocessing, loss function, training details, hyper-parameters, etc. The description should also include a link to a code repository with complete and detailed instructions so that the results obtained can be reproduced. After your solution has been validated, you may be asked to discuss your results via a recorded call or panel call with the competition sponsors, which call may take the form of a panel with the attendance of other winners.

1. EXTERNAL DATA AND TOOLS
a. You may use data other than the Competition Data (“External Data”) to develop and test your Submissions. However, you will ensure the External Data is either publicly available and equally accessible to use by all Participants of the Competition for purposes of the competition at no cost to the other Participants, or satisfies the Reasonableness criteria as outlined in Section 2.6.b below. The ability to use External Data under this Section does not limit your other obligations under these Competition Rules, including but not limited to Section 2.8 (Winners Obligations).

b. The use of external data and models is acceptable unless specifically prohibited by the Host. Because of the potential costs or restrictions (e.g., “geo restrictions”) associated with obtaining rights to use external data or certain software and associated tools, their use must be “reasonably accessible to all” and of “minimal cost”. Also, regardless of the cost challenges as they might affect all Participants during the course of the competition, the costs of potentially procuring a license for software used to generate a Submission, must also be considered. The Host will employ an assessment of whether or not the following criteria can exclude the use of the particular LLM, data set(s), or tool(s):

Are Participants being excluded from a competition because of the "excessive" costs for access to certain LLMs, external data, or tools that might be used by other Participants. The Host will assess the excessive cost concern by applying a “Reasonableness” standard (the “Reasonableness Standard”). The Reasonableness Standard will be determined and applied by the Host in light of things like cost thresholds and accessibility.

By way of example only, a small subscription charge to use additional elements of a large language model such as Gemini Advanced are acceptable if meeting the Reasonableness Standard of Sec. 8.2. Purchasing a license to use a proprietary dataset that exceeds the cost of a prize in the competition would not be considered reasonable.

c. Automated Machine Learning Tools (“AMLT”)

Individual Participants and Teams may use automated machine learning tool(s) (“AMLT”) (e.g., Google toML, H2O Driverless AI, etc.) to create a Submission, provided that the Participant or Team ensures that they have an appropriate license to the AMLT such that they are able to comply with the Competition Rules.
7. ELIGIBILITY
a. Unless otherwise stated in the Competition-Specific Rules above or prohibited by internal policies of the Competition Entities, employees, interns, contractors, officers and directors of Competition Entities may enter and participate in the Competition, but are not eligible to win any Prizes. "Competition Entities" means the Competition Sponsor, Kaggle Inc., and their respective parent companies, subsidiaries and affiliates. If you are such a Participant from a Competition Entity, you are subject to all applicable internal policies of your employer with respect to your participation.

1. WINNER’S OBLIGATIONS
a. As a condition to being awarded a Prize, a Prize winner must fulfill the following obligations:

Deliver to the Competition Sponsor the final model's software code, including model weights, as used to generate the winning Submission and associated documentation. The delivered software code should follow these documentation guidelines, must be capable of generating the winning Submission, and contain a description of resources required to build and/or run the executable code successfully. For avoidance of doubt, delivered software code should include training code, inference code, model weights, and a description of the required computational environment. The computational environment can take the form of a pip requirements.txt with one of: 1) the corresponding kaggle image or a 2) a dockerfile used to build the image. The model weights should be provided as a public kaggle dataset so it is both publicly accessible and linked to the inference/submission code. For Hackathons, the Submission deliverables will be as described on the Competition Website, which may be information or materials that are not software code.
a. To the extent that the final model’s software code includes generally commercially available software that is not owned by you, but that can be procured by the Competition Sponsor without undue expense, then instead of delivering the code for that software to the Competition Sponsor, you must identify that software, method for procuring it, and any parameters or other information necessary to replicate the winning Submission; Individual Participants and Teams who create a Submission using an AMLT may win a Prize. However, for clarity, the potential winner’s Submission must still meet the requirements of these Rules, including but not limited to Section 2.5 (Winners License), Section 2.8 (Winners Obligations), and Section 3.14 (Warranty, Indemnity, and Release).

b. Individual Participants and Teams who create a Submission using an AMLT may win a Prize. However, for clarity, the potential winner’s Submission must still meet the requirements of these Rules,

Grant to the Competition Sponsor the license to the winning Submission stated in the Competition Specific Rules above, and represent that you have the unrestricted right to grant that license;

Sign and return all Prize acceptance documents as may be required by Competition Sponsor or Kaggle, including without limitation: (a) eligibility certifications; (b) licenses, releases and other agreements required under the Rules; and (c) U.S. tax forms (such as IRS Form W-9 if U.S. resident, IRS Form W-8BEN if foreign resident, or future equivalents).

6. DETERMINING WINNERS
a. Each Submission will be scored and/or ranked by the evaluation metric, or Evaluation Rubric (in the case of Hackathon Competitions),stated on the Competition Website. During the Competition Period, the current ranking will be visible on the Competition Website's Public Leaderboard. The potential winner(s) are determined solely by the leaderboard ranking on the Private Leaderboard, subject to compliance with these Rules. The Public Leaderboard will be based on the public test set and the Private Leaderboard will be based on the private test set. There will be no leaderboards for Hackathon Competitions. b. In the event of a tie, the Submission that was entered first to the Competition will be the winner. In the event a potential winner is disqualified for any reason, the Submission that received the next highest score rank will be chosen as the potential winner. For Hackathon Competitions, each of the top Submissions will get a unique ranking and there will be no tiebreakers.

In case (i) and (ii) above Kaggle may disqualify the Participant. However, in case (ii) above, if requested by Kaggle, such potential winner may provide code and documentation to verify the Participant’s compliance with these Rules. If the potential winner provides code and documentation to the satisfaction of Kaggle, the Participant will not be disqualified pursuant to this paragraph. d. Competition Sponsor reserves the right to disqualify any Participant from the Competition if the Competition Sponsor reasonably believes that the Participant has attempted to undermine the legitimate operation of the Competition by cheating, deception, or other unfair playing practices or abuses, threatens or harasses any other Participants, Competition Sponsor or Kaggle. e. A disqualified Participant may be removed from the Competition leaderboard, at Kaggle's sole discretion. RSNA Knee Abnormality Detection
Create a model that can detect knee abnormalities based on multimodal imaging data

RSNA Knee Abnormality Detection

Join Competition
Po-Hao "Howard" Chen · Posted 2 months ago
·
Competition Host
Knee Abnormality Detection AI Challenge Overview
Courtesy of Dr. Jacob Kazam

Overview of the Clinical Problem
The 2026 RSNA Knee Abnormality Detection AI Challenge asks participants to build machine learning models that detect a defined set of clinically important abnormalities on knee MRI examinations. It is the first RSNA AI Challenge to pair every imaging study with the text of its original radiology report, and to do so across a large, multilingual, international dataset. Models may learn from both the images and the report text, reflecting how knee MRI is actually interpreted and communicated in clinical practice.

The knee is one of the most frequently injured and most frequently imaged joints in the body. Knee disorders are a major and growing cause of pain and disability worldwide, affecting athletes, manual workers, and older adults alike. The knee is the most common site of osteoarthritis, which affects nearly a quarter of adults aged 40 and older, an estimated 654 million people worldwide. Acute injuries are also common: knee injuries account for an estimated 15 to 40 percent of all sports-related injuries.

Several structures are commonly involved. Anterior cruciate ligament (ACL) tears are among the most frequent sports injuries, often occurring during sudden pivots or changes of direction and leading to immediate instability. Medial collateral ligament (MCL) injuries typically result from outward (valgus) stress on the knee and frequently accompany other structural damage. Meniscal tears, which may be traumatic or degenerative, disrupt the joint’s shock absorption and mechanics, producing pain, locking, and functional limitation. Cartilage loss, joint effusion, synovitis, bone contusions, fractures, and Baker (popliteal) cysts are additional findings that cause symptoms and influence management.

MRI is central to evaluating the knee because it depicts all of these structures, including ligaments, menisci, articular cartilage, and bone, without ionizing radiation, providing a comprehensive whole-joint assessment that other modalities cannot match. However, MRI interpretation varies across readers and practice settings, and access to subspecialty-trained musculoskeletal (MSK) radiologists is limited in many parts of the world. Reliable, consistent automated detection of knee abnormalities could help extend expert-level interpretation, reduce variability, and support more timely care.

The goal of this competition is to develop machine learning models that detect twelve clinically important knee abnormalities, learning from a large international dataset of knee MRI examinations paired with their original, multilingual radiology reports.

Anatomical Overview
The knee is a synovial hinge joint formed by three bones: the femur (thigh bone) above, the tibia (shin bone) below, and the patella (kneecap) in front. The ends of these bones are covered by articular cartilage, a smooth layer that allows near-frictionless motion and distributes load. The joint is enclosed by a capsule lined with synovium, which produces lubricating fluid; an abnormal excess of this fluid is termed a joint effusion, and inflammation of the synovium is termed synovitis.

Knee anatomy: anterior view (top) and lateral view (bottom), showing the femur, tibia, patella, cruciate ligaments, and meniscus. Illustrations by BruceBlaus, CC BY 3.0, via Wikimedia Commons.

For the purposes of this challenge, the knee is considered in three compartments. The medial compartment lies between the medial femoral condyle and the medial tibial plateau, on the inner side of the knee. The lateral compartment lies between the lateral femoral condyle and the lateral tibial plateau, on the outer side. The patellofemoral compartment lies between the patella and the femoral trochlea, the groove on the front of the femur. Osteoarthritis, or cartilage loss, is assessed separately in each of these three compartments.

Four ligaments stabilize the knee. The anterior cruciate ligament (ACL) and posterior cruciate ligament (PCL) lie within the joint, in the intercondylar notch, and control front-to-back stability and rotation. The medial collateral ligament (MCL) and lateral collateral ligament (LCL) run along the inner and outer sides of the knee and resist side-to-side stress. This challenge focuses on tears of the ACL and the MCL.

Between the femur and tibia sit two menisci, the medial meniscus and the lateral meniscus, C-shaped wedges of fibrocartilage that cushion the joint, absorb shock, and improve the fit between the rounded femur and the relatively flat tibia. Tears of the medial and lateral meniscus are evaluated separately.

Other relevant structures include the bone marrow within the femur, tibia, and patella, where a bruise from impact is called a bone contusion (or bone marrow edema) and a break in the bone is a fracture; and the soft tissues behind the knee, where a fluid-filled outpouching of the joint lining is called a Baker, or popliteal, cyst.

Imaging Overview
Several modalities can image the knee. Radiographs (X-rays) show bones and joint-space narrowing but cannot directly visualize ligaments, menisci, or cartilage. Ultrasound and CT have specific roles but offer limited soft-tissue contrast for the internal structures of the joint. Magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) is the reference standard for evaluating internal derangement of the knee because it provides excellent soft-tissue contrast, depicts ligaments, menisci, cartilage, and bone marrow simultaneously, and does not use ionizing radiation.

A knee MRI examination is not a single image but a set of series, each acquired with a particular pulse sequence and in a particular imaging plane. The three standard planes are axial (cross-sectional, viewed as if looking up through the leg), coronal (a front-facing, side-to-side view), and sagittal (a side-profile, front-to-back view). Different structures are best seen in different planes; for example, the cruciate ligaments and menisci are well evaluated on sagittal and coronal images, and the patellofemoral cartilage on axial images.

Sequences differ in how they weight tissue signal. Fluid-sensitive sequences, such as proton-density or T2-weighted images, often with fat suppression, make edema, effusion, and tears appear bright and are central to detecting most abnormalities; a meniscal tear, for instance, appears as abnormally increased signal that reaches the surface of the meniscus on more than one image. Other sequences emphasize anatomy or cartilage detail. Interpreting a study therefore requires integrating information across multiple series, planes, and slices for the same knee, an inherently three-dimensional task.

Sagittal proton-density, fat-suppressed (fluid-sensitive) MRI of the knee, the type of sequence on which most abnormalities are detected. Image by Ptrump16, CC BY-SA 4.0, via Wikimedia Commons.

For the purpose of the 2026 Knee Abnormality Challenge, a “fluid sensitive” sequence refers to one in which edema, hemorrhage, and other types of fluid appear bright and fat is suppressed in some way.

Because findings can be subtle and the criteria for what counts as clinically significant are nuanced, interpretation varies between readers. This variability, combined with limited access to subspecialty MSK radiologists, motivates the development of consistent automated tools.

Label Description
Models are evaluated on twelve binary labels, each indicating the presence or absence of a specific finding in the imaged knee. The labels, and the criteria used by the annotating radiologists, are summarized below. In each case, ambiguous or borderline findings (“on the fence”) were graded as negative to favor specificity.

ACL tear: A high-grade partial or full-thickness tear of the anterior cruciate ligament, meaning complete discontinuity of the ligament, or more than 50 percent of fibers disrupted, with or without secondary signs such as characteristic pivot-shift bone contusions. Mild signal change, degeneration, or thickening without discontinuity is graded negative.

MCL tear: A high-grade partial or complete acute tear of the medial collateral ligament, with disrupted fibers and edema within and adjacent to the ligament. Low-grade sprains and chronic or remote stress changes are graded negative.

Medial meniscus tear: Abnormal signal that definitely contacts the meniscal surface on at least two images, or a morphologic abnormality such as a truncated, diminutive, or displaced fragment, involving the medial meniscus. Intrasubstance degeneration that does not reach the surface is negative.

Lateral meniscus tear: The same criteria applied to the lateral meniscus.

Medial compartment osteoarthritis: A moderate or large area (roughly 1 cm or greater) of high-grade cartilage loss, defined as greater than 50 percent of cartilage thickness, in the medial compartment, with or without underlying subchondral marrow changes.

Lateral compartment osteoarthritis: The same criteria applied to the lateral compartment.

Patellofemoral compartment osteoarthritis: The same criteria applied to the patellofemoral compartment.

Joint effusion: A moderate or large amount of fluid distending the joint.

Synovitis: Inflammation and thickening of the synovial lining of the joint.

Baker (popliteal) cyst: A moderate or large fluid collection in the characteristic location behind the knee.

Contusion: A bone contusion, seen as bone marrow edema-like signal from impact, without a discrete fracture line.

Acute fracture: An acute cortical break or fracture line.

Each study in the annotated reference set was independently labeled by two subspecialty-trained MSK radiologists, with disagreements adjudicated by a third radiologist to produce a single consensus ground truth. Labels are assigned at the level of the whole examination, for a single knee.

Examples
The following annotated examples, drawn from the challenge’s image annotation guidelines, illustrate representative positive and negative cases for several of the labels. In each figure, yellow arrows and arrowheads mark the findings described in the caption.

Use of Commercially Hosted LLMs
Use of commercially hosted LLMs and other external inference services is permitted, provided that the service and method of use otherwise comply with the Competition Rules, including requirements that external data, models, software, and associated tools be reasonably accessible to all participants and of minimal cost.

In other words, for purposes of this competition, submitting Competition Data, including report text, to an external LLM or API for inference or other computational processing (for example, extracting labels from reports) will not, by itself, be considered prohibited PRIVATE SHARING of Competition Data outside the Team.

Please refer to the RSNA Knee Abnormality Detection Competition Rules. This is in accordance to Section 2.6.b, EXTERNAL DATA AND TOOLS.

The PRIVATE SHARING restriction is intended to prohibit sharing Competition Data, code, or competition-specific work product with other participants, teams, or third parties for collaboration or competitive use outside the registered Team.

Participants remain responsible for ensuring that any external service complies with all applicable Competition Rules and the service's terms of use, including the requirements for reasonable accessibility and minimal cost.

The Competition Host reserves the right to determine whether a particular service, model, or configuration is reasonably accessible, is prohibitively costly, or otherwise creates an unfair competitive advantage.

React
Po-Hao "Howard" Chen
Competition Host
Posted a month ago

From the competition’s perspective, use of these datasets would be considered non-commercial; receipt of competition prize money does not, by itself, make their use a commercial sale or commercial exploitation. These datasets would not be excluded by the competition rules solely because their licenses restrict use to non-commercial purposes.

A separate consideration is whether an external dataset is reasonably accessible to all competitors. Datasets that can be accessed through a straightforward registration or click-through data-use agreement would generally satisfy this requirement. By contrast, datasets requiring institution-specific approvals, negotiated written agreements, IRB approval, lengthy credentialing, or other substantial administrative steps may present a meaningful accessibility barrier, particularly given the remaining competition timeline. Such requirements could therefore affect whether a dataset is considered allowable under the competition’s external-data rules, independent of whether its use is characterized as commercial or non-commercial.

Our job (Host) is tough here because RSNA and Kaggle do not own or control these external datasets and cannot interpret or waive their individual data-use agreements, expedite requests and so on, on behalf of the dataset owners. Teams remain responsible for ensuring that their intended use, including participation in a prize-bearing competition and any required release of code or models, is permitted under the applicable dataset license or agreement.

Therefore, to the extent the question concerns the competition rules, readily accessible datasets with non-commercial restrictions are not prohibited on that basis alone. Dataset-specific licensing obligations remain the responsibility of each team, and datasets involving substantial approval or access hurdles may need to be considered separately under the competition’s equal-accessibility requirements.

Sorry for the verbose response. I hope it's at least a little helpful…

RSNA Knee Abnormality Detection
Create a model that can detect knee abnormalities based on multimodal imaging data

This competition requires identity verification
To submit to this competition, you'll need to verify your identity. Learn More

Verify now
Overview
A single knee scan can reveal a dozen different problems. In this competition, you are tasked to build machine learning models that detect a defined set of clinically important abnormalities on knee MRI examinations.

Start

2 months ago
Close

11 days to go
Merger & Entry
Description
The knee is the most commonly injured and imaged joint in the body. Osteoarthritis alone affects an estimated 654 million people worldwide, while acute knee injuries account for 15 to 40 percent of all sports-related trauma. MRIs show clinicians ligaments, cartilage, menisci, and bone in detail, without exposing patients to radiation.

Reading those scans isn’t always straightforward. ACL and MCL tears, meniscal damage, cartilage loss, fractures, and other abnormalities can be subtle, and radiologists don’t always interpret them the same way. Access to musculoskeletal radiologists is also limited, especially outside major medical centers, leading to delays and inconsistent diagnoses.

In this competition, you will develop multimodal machine learning models to detect twelve clinically important knee abnormalities. You'll work with the first RSNA AI Challenge dataset that pairs every imaging study with its original radiology report, enabling your models to learn from both visual scans and written diagnostic text.

High-performing models can act as robust decision support tools, delivering the accuracy, consistency, and speed needed to elevate expert-level knee MRI interpretation and improve care across disparate clinic settings.

Evaluation
Submissions are evaluated by the average area under the ROC curve between the predicted confidence scores and the observed targets across the twelve targets:

The final score is, in other words, the macro-averaged AUC ROC.

Submission File
For each row in the test set, you must predict a confidence score for each of the twelve target labels. The file should contain a header and have the following format:

StudyInstanceUID,ACL,MCL,Medial Meniscus,Lateral Meniscus,Medial OA,Lateral OA,PF OA,Effusion,Synovitis,Baker's,Contusion,Fracture
<uid_1>,0.5,0.5,0.5,0.5,0.5,0.5,0.5,0.5,0.5,0.5,0.5,0.5
<uid_2>,0.5,0.5,0.5,0.5,0.5,0.5,0.5,0.5,0.5,0.5,0.5,0.5
...
Timeline
July 30, 2026 - Start Date.
October 15, 2026 - Entry Deadline. You must accept the competition rules before this date in order to compete.
October 15, 2026 - Team Merger Deadline. This is the last day participants may join or merge teams.
October 22, 2026 - Final Submission Deadline.
November 5, 2026 - Winners' Requirement Deadline. This is the deadline for winners to submit to the host/Kaggle their training code, video and method description.
All deadlines are at 11:59 PM UTC on the corresponding day unless otherwise noted. The competition organizers reserve the right to update the contest timeline if they deem it necessary.

Prizes
Main Leaderboard
First Prize: $9,000
Second Prize: $7,000
Third Prize: $6,500
Fourth Prize: $6,000
Fifth Prize: $5,500
Sixth Prize: $5,000
Seventh Prize: $5,000
Eighth Prize: $5,000
Ninth Prize: $5,000
Tenth Prize: $5,000

Efficiency Track
First Efficiency Prize: $7,000
Second Efficiency Prize: $6,000
Third Efficiency Prize: $5,000

Because this competition is being hosted in coordination with the Radiological Society of North America (RSNA) Annual Meeting, winners will be invited and strongly encouraged to attend the AI Challenge Recognition Event with waived fee, contingent on review of solution and fulfillment of winners' obligations.

Note that, per the competition rules, in addition to the standard Kaggle Winners' Obligations (open-source licensing requirements, solution packaging/delivery, presentation to host), the host team also asks that you:

(i) create a short video presenting your approach and solution, and

(ii) publish a link to your open sourced code and the weights on the competition forum

(iii) Share final version of model as publicly available for open distribution and validation. Please see <https://www.kaggle.com/models/tom99763/9th-place-models-rsna-iad/PyTorch/default> as an example.

Code Requirements

Submissions to this competition must be made through Notebooks. In order for the "Submit" button to be active after a commit, the following conditions must be met:

CPU Notebook <= 9 hours run-time
GPU Notebook <= 9 hours run-time
Internet access disabled
Freely & publicly available external data is allowed, including pre-trained models
Submission file must be named submission.csv
Please see the Code Competition FAQ for more information on how to submit. And review the code debugging doc if you are encountering submission errors.

Efficiency Prize Evaluation
Efficiency Prize
We are hosting a second track that focuses on model efficiency, because highly accurate models are often computationally heavy.

For the Efficiency Prize, we will evaluate submissions on both runtime and predictive performance.

To be eligible for an Efficiency Prize, a submission:

Must be among the submissions selected by a team for the Leaderboard Prize, or else among those submissions automatically selected under the conditions described in the My Submissions tab.
Must be ranked on the Private Leaderboard higher than the sample_submission.csv benchmark.
All submissions meeting these conditions will be considered for the Efficiency Prize. A submission may be eligible for both the Leaderboard Prize and the Efficiency Prize.

An Efficiency Prize will be awarded to eligible submissions according to how they are ranked by the following evaluation metric on the private test data. See the Prizes tab for the prize awarded to each rank. More details may be posted via discussion forum updates.

Efficiency Score

where

 of all submissions on the Private Leaderboard, and
 is the number of seconds it takes for the submission to be evaluated. The objective is to minimize the efficiency score.

During the training period of the competition, you may see a leaderboard for the public test data in the following notebook, updated daily: Efficiency Leaderboard. After the competition ends, we will update this leaderboard with efficiency scores on the private data. During the training period, this leaderboard will show only the rank of each team, but not the complete score.

Pillar-0 Knee MRI Fine-tune
fabotelli/pillar0-knee-finetune

Pillar-0 BreastMRI foundation model fine-tuned on RSNA 2026 knee MRI

Pillar-0 Knee MRI Fine-tune

Download

Code
Model Details
Full fine-tune of YalaLab's Pillar-0 (BreastMRI) radiology foundation model on knee MRI from the RSNA 2026 Knee Abnormality Detection competition. Published as an honest cross-anatomy transfer study: 58-study expert holdout macro-AUC 0.797 (frozen probe 0.697; task-specific CNN teachers 0.89-0.90). Strong on Effusion (0.965) and Medial OA (0.941); the domain gap concentrates in ligament/meniscus fine structure. ECL-2.0, attribution to YalaLab Pillar-0.

New Notebook
About Variation
Fine-Tunable
Yes

License
Other (specified in description)

Atlas backbone (Pillar-0 BreastMRI) + Linear(1152,12) head, 2-epoch full fine-tune at [192,384,384], soft-BCE on LLM rubric labels. Gold-58 macro-AUC 0.797.

Example Use
state = torch.load('pillar_ft_a.final.pt'); load state['model'] into the upstream CLIPMultimodalAtlas (YalaLab/pillar-pretrain), state['head'] into torch.nn.Linear(1152,12). Input: volume [D,256,256] -> /255 -> z-score -> trilinear to [192,384,384] -> repeat to 3ch -> extract_vision_feats -> head -> sigmoid. Study prob = mean over series.

File Explorer
README.md(2.82 kB)
Pillar-0 (BreastMRI) → Knee MRI fine-tune — RSNA 2026 Knee Abnormality Detection
A full fine-tune of the Pillar-0 BreastMRI radiology foundation model (YalaLab/pillar-0, code, ECL-2.0) on knee MRI from the RSNA 2026 Knee Abnormality Detection competition — published as an honest cross-anatomy transfer study, including where it falls short.

TL;DR
Breast-MRI volumetric pretraining transfers partially to knee MRI: two epochs of full fine-tuning lift gold-set macro-AUC from 0.697 (frozen linear probe) to 0.797, with strong columns on effusion/OA-adjacent targets — but it stays ~0.10 below task-specific 2.5D CNN teachers (0.89–0.90) trained on the same labels. Ligament/meniscus fine structure is where the domain gap concentrates.

Results (58-study expert-annotated holdout, AUC)
Target AUC
ACL 0.773
MCL 0.630
Medial Meniscus 0.750
Lateral Meniscus 0.781
Medial OA 0.941
Lateral OA 0.774
PF OA 0.758
Effusion 0.965
Synovitis 0.821
Baker's 0.842
Contusion 0.756
Fracture 0.775
Macro 0.797
Reference points on the same holdout: frozen Pillar-0 probe 0.697; DINOv3- package OOF 0.854; task-specific fine-tuned teachers 0.89–0.90.

Training recipe
Adapter: uint8 volume [D,256,256] → /255 → per-volume z-score → GPU trilinear to the checkpoint's rigid [192,384,384] grid → channel-replicate ×3 → extract_vision_feats → Linear(1152→12).
Loss: soft BCE against LLM-extracted rubric labels (per-study confidences broadcast to series; series capped at 4/study, seed 0).
Optim: AdamW, split LRs (backbone 1e-5, head 1e-3), OneCycle, bf16, grad-clip 1.0, effective batch 6, 2 epochs on one NVIDIA B200 (~70 min).
Study-level prediction = mean sigmoid over all series of the study.
The 58 holdout studies were excluded from training.
Files
pillar_ft_a.final.pt — {"model": <Pillar-0 Atlas backbone state_dict>, "head": <Linear(1152,12) state_dict>, "ep": 1}. Load the backbone with the upstream CLIPMultimodalAtlas class from the Pillar-0 release, then attach the linear head; input adapter as above.
LICENSE — ECL-2.0, inherited from the base model.
Attribution & license
Base model: Pillar-0 (Breast MRI) by YalaLab (Atlas vision backbone, contrastively aligned with Qwen3-Embedding-8B), released under the Educational Community License 2.0. This derivative retains ECL-2.0. If you use this, please cite the Pillar-0 paper ("Pillar-0: A New Frontier for Radiology Foundation Models") and credit the base model authors.

Fine-tune, evaluation, and card by fabotelli (RSNA 2026 knee competition).

Qwen2.5-32B Weak Labels for RSNA Knee Abnormality
Qwen2.5-32B v2 weak labels; macro ROC AUC 0.8686; Qwen3-8B v1 deprecated

About Dataset
RSNA Knee Abnormality Weak Labels — Qwen2.5-32B (v2)
2026-09 update: this dataset is now generated with Qwen/Qwen2.5-32B-Instruct using a rewritten v2 prompt. The old Qwen3-8B output is kept below (qwen_knee_weak_labels.csv) for reproducibility only and is deprecated: its data card reported a pooled ROC AUC (0.851) instead of the competition's macro ROC AUC, and its v1 prompt over-called Effusion and under-called Synovitis.

Files
File Description
weak_labels_v2_filled.csv Production file — 4,407 studies, 4,407/4,407 status=ok (0 errors). Raw Qwen2.5-32B output + conservative co-occurrence priors (see below).
weak_labels_v2_raw.csv Raw Qwen2.5-32B output, identical schema, no post-processing.
qwen_knee_weak_labels.csv Deprecated Qwen3-8B v1 output (kept for reference only).
Qwen2.5-32BGenerator.ipynb Generator notebook (Qwen2.5-32B, v2 prompt).
Qwen3-8BGenerator.ipynb Deprecated v1 generator (kept for reference only).
Both v2 files keep the exact same column schema as the old v1 file, so downstream pipelines (e.g. build_targets()) keep working unchanged.

Validation
Predictions were matched to the 58 labeled studies using StudyInstanceUID (58 × 12 = 696 label decisions). For v2 labels, <label>__confidence is the model's calibrated P(present) and is used directly as the ROC score (no label flip — v1 semantics were "confidence in the predicted class" and are not comparable).

Label Accuracy ROC AUC
ACL 87.9% 0.981
MCL 84.5% 0.971
Medial Meniscus 82.8% 0.897
Lateral Meniscus 82.8% 0.860
Medial OA 82.8% 0.916
Lateral OA 84.5% 0.795
PF OA 75.9% 0.840
Effusion 75.9% 0.849
Synovitis 70.7% 0.789
Baker's 87.9% 0.903
Contusion 67.2% 0.745
Fracture 84.5% 0.876
Macro ROC AUC (—) — 0.8686
Macro is the competition metric. Per-target macro AUC of the raw (unfilled) file: 0.8521.
Pooled ROC AUC (reference only, not the competition metric): 0.8754 (filled) / 0.8558 (raw). The deprecated v1 card reported its pooled value (0.851) as if it were the metric.
Decision-level overall accuracy at the __label threshold: 80.6% (561/696).
Co-occurrence filling (exact operation)
weak_labels_v2_filled.csv is produced from weak_labels_v2_raw.csv by three conservative rules that touch only cells the report did not mention (<target>__mentioned == 0) and whose partner finding has __confidence > 0.5:

Apply when target unmentioned AND partner conf > 0.5 Fill value Empirical P(target ∣ partner) Rationale
PF OA ← Medial OA 0.60 ~0.70 OA is often multi-compartment
Synovitis ← Medial OA 0.60 ~0.74 synovitis is a common OA comorbidity
Synovitis ← Effusion 0.55 ~0.63 validated flagship rule
Operation applied per cell: <target>__confidence = max(<target>__confidence, fill value). The label, mentioned and negated columns are never modified. Cells touched on the 4,407-study file: PF OA 385, Synovitis (via Medial OA) 996, Synovitis (via Effusion) 739. Effect on the 58 gold studies: macro ROC AUC 0.8521 → 0.8686.

Generation
Model: Qwen/Qwen2.5-32B-Instruct, 4-bit NF4 (bitsandbytes, double quant), bf16 compute where available, greedy decoding, batch size 4
GPU: cloud NVIDIA RTX 4090 (24 GB) — ~20 GB VRAM needed at 4-bit; also runs on Kaggle T4 x2 via device_map="auto"
Generator: Qwen2.5-32BGenerator.ipynb / notebook
Input studies: 4,407
Evaluated studies: 58 with available ground-truth labels
Evaluated label instances: 696
v2 prompt changes vs v1 (Qwen3-8B, deprecated)
Effusion (rewritten — was badly over-called, prevalence 0.88 vs gold 0.60): only effusions of a degree worth reporting count; small / trace / incidental fluid leans negative (probability 0.30–0.45).
Synovitis (rewritten — was badly under-called, sensitivity 48%): explicitly counts the classic indirect descriptions — Hoffa fat-pad impingement / infrapatellar oedema, plica syndrome, synovial thickening/hypertrophy — not just the word "synovitis".
Probability semantics: the model returns a calibrated, granular P(present) (probability), stored in <label>__confidence. (In v1 it was "confidence in the predicted class", which required the label flip when scoring and made pooled ROC numbers meaningless.)
Abnormality Labels
Label Meaning
ACL Anterior cruciate ligament abnormality
MCL Medial collateral ligament abnormality
Medial Meniscus Medial meniscus abnormality
Lateral Meniscus Lateral meniscus abnormality
Medial OA Medial compartment osteoarthritis
Lateral OA Lateral compartment osteoarthritis
PF OA Patellofemoral osteoarthritis
Effusion Knee joint effusion
Synovitis Synovial inflammation
Baker's Baker's cyst
Contusion Contusion
Fracture Fracture
Columns
StudyInstanceUID — unique identifier for the study
Report — original radiology report
qwen_raw — raw Qwen2.5-32B output
status — generation status
error — generation error, if any
generation_seconds — generation time in seconds
<label>__label — predicted label: 1 = abnormal/present, 0 = normal/absent
<label>__confidence — v2: calibrated P(present) from the model (use directly as score / soft label). Deprecated v1 file: confidence in the predicted class.
<label>__mentioned — whether the abnormality was mentioned in the report: 1 = yes, 0 = no
<label>__negated — whether the finding was negated: 1 = yes, 0 = no
The four <label>__* columns are provided for each of the 12 abnormalities above.

About this file

Generator Notebook

{
 "cells": [
  {
   "cell_type": "markdown",
   "metadata": {},
   "source": [
    "# RSNA Knee MRI — Weak Labeling with Qwen2.5-32B\n",
    "\n",
    "This notebook documents the __Qwen/Qwen2.5-32B-Instruct__ zero-shot structured-extraction run that produced the current weak labels for the RSNA Knee Abnormality Detection competition. It replaces the deprecated Qwen3-8B pipeline, whose data card reported a __pooled__ ROC AUC (0.851) instead of the competition's __macro__ ROC AUC, and whose v1 prompt over-called Effusion (predicted prevalence 0.88 vs 0.60 on the gold studies) and under-called Synovitis (sensitivity 48%).\n",
    "\n",
    "The model reads each knee MRI report and classifies the 12 target abnormalities (ACL, MCL, menisci, OA compartments, effusion, synovitis, Baker's cyst, contusion, fracture), returning per-target `label`, a calibrated `probability` (stored in the `__confidence` column for schema compatibility), `mentioned` and `negated`.\n",
    "\n",
    "__Flow:__ load data → load Qwen2.5-32B (4-bit) → v2 prompt + strict JSON parser → batched inference → conservative co-occurrence fill → __macro ROC AUC__ validation on the 58 ground-truth studies.\n",
    "\n",
    "> The official full run was executed on a cloud __RTX 4090 (24 GB)__ — Qwen2.5-32B at 4-bit needs ~20 GB of VRAM, so it does not fit on one 16 GB Kaggle GPU (it does fit on Kaggle T4 x2 via `device_map=\"auto\"`, or any 24 GB+ GPU). Both output files (`weak_labels_v2_raw.csv` and `weak_labels_v2_filled.csv`) are published in the output dataset; to reproduce the run flip `RUN_GENERATION = True` in the __Setup & configuration__ cell (§1) and run on a GPU.\n"
   ]
  },

   "cell_type": "markdown",
   "metadata": {},

    "Model performance is evaluated on the __58 labeled cases__ across the 12 target findings (__696 target decisions__), using the production file `weak_labels_v2_filled.csv`. The headline metric is the competition metric __macro ROC AUC__.\n",
    "\n",
    "> The deprecated Qwen3-8B data card reported an \"Overall ROC AUC 0.851\" — that was the *pooled* ROC AUC over all 696 decisions concatenated into one curve, which is __not__ the competition metric. The macro ROC AUC of those same v1 decisions was ≈0.8295. All numbers below are macro-based / per-target, so they are directly comparable to competition scores.\n",

    "\n",
    "Rules apply only to cells the report does __not__ mention (`{target}__mentioned == 0`) whose partner has confidence > 0.5; they raise `__confidence` to `max(current, fill)` and never touch `label`/`mentioned`/`negated`. Cells touched on the 4,407-study file: PF OA 385, Synovitis (via Medial OA) 996, Synovitis (via Effusion) 739.\n",

    "| ---------- | ---: | ----------: |\n",

    "*__Hardest targets by AUC:__ Contusion (0.745), Synovitis (0.789), Lateral OA (0.795).\n",
    "* __Confidence is now a granular probability__ (e.g. Effusion mean 0.63, ACL 0.50) rather than v1's high self-reported certainty — this is what makes the ROC scores meaningful.\n",
    "\n",
    "Overall the v2 labels are substantially better ranked (macro AUC 0.8686) and much better calibrated on Effusion and Synovitis than the deprecated v1 labels.\n"

In this Kaggle notebook they come from the competition input;\n# point this at a local copy when running outside Kaggle.\nTRAIN_CSV = \"/kaggle/input/competitions/rsna-knee-abnormality-detection/train.csv\"\n\n# Outputs (same schema as the official files)\nRAW_OUT = \"/kaggle/working/weak_labels_v2_raw.csv\"\nFILL_OUT = \"/kaggle/working/weak_labels_v2_filled.csv\"\n\n# Official outputs shipped in the dataset (used by the validation cell)\n_DATA_DIR = \"/kaggle/input/rsna-knee-abnormality-qwen3-8b-weak-labels\"\nOFFICIAL_RAW = f\"{_DATA_DIR}/weak_labels_v2_raw.csv\"\nOFFICIAL_FILL = f\"{_DATA_DIR}/weak_labels_v2_filled.csv\"\n\nprint(f\"model        : {MODEL_ID} ({QUANT})\")\nprint(f\"generate     : RUN_GENERATION={RUN_GENERATION}, LIMIT={LIMIT}\")\nprint(f\"train.csv    : {TRAIN_CSV}\")\nprint(f\"official raw : {os.path.exists(OFFICIAL_RAW)}  (dataset mounted)\")\nprint(f\"official fill: {os.path.exists(OFFICIAL_FILL)}  (dataset mounted)\")\n"
